I have stuff in too many places—Slack, sticky notes, email, my phone. Work tickets, house repair things, budget reminders… it gets scattered, and then I spend more time figuring out what I was supposed to do than just doing it.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel the need to be physically active, like running, climbing, or engaging in other intense physical activities?\n\nPatient: Not like running or climbing, no. It’s more… I can be sitting still, but inside I feel kind of wound up, like I need to move or switch tasks or do something with my hands. Otherwise I get really uncomfortable.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself easily distracted by things around you?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure. I mean… I notice things, but I don’t know if it’s more than most people. It depends on the situation, I guess.\n\nDoctor: Have you always felt this way, even when you were a kid?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I think so. I mean, in elementary school I remember teachers moving my desk sometimes, or telling me to stay seated. I’d pop up a lot, talk before they called on me, or rush through work even when I knew how to do it.\n\nDoctor: Do you often have strong emotional reactions to situations?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… not especially, I guess. I can get irritated sometimes, but I wouldn’t say I’ve really noticed big emotional reactions.\n\nDoctor: Have these difficulties you're describing caused any problems for you at work, at home, or in your relationships with others?\n\nPatient: Yeah… a little. It’s not like everything is falling apart, but at work and at home people have been having to check in with me more. I can tell it’s causing some friction. I worry they’re starting to see me as the unreliable one with planning things, even though I’m trying.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced these difficulties consistently throughout your childhood and into adulthood?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think it’s been pretty consistent. Like I said, even in elementary school teachers would move my desk or remind me to stay seated because I kept popping up or talking before I was called on. I’d also rush through work even when I understood it. It’s sort of always been there, just looks a little different now.\n\nDoctor: As a child, did you engage in a lot of physical activity, like running around or climbing, more than other kids?\n\nPatient: I guess not so much climbing or anything like that… but I do remember feeling like I had to move a lot. I’d pop up a lot, talk before they called on me, or rush through work even when I knew how to do it.\n\nDoctor: Do you often have strong emotional reactions to situations?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… not especially, I guess. I can get irritated sometimes, but I wouldn’t say I’ve really noticed big emotional reactions.\n\nDoctor: Have these difficulties you're describing caused any problems for you at work, at home, or in your relationships with others?\n\nPatient: Yeah… a little. It’s not like everything is falling apart, but at work and at home people have been having to check in with me more. I can tell it’s causing some friction. I worry they’re starting to see me as the unreliable one with planning things, even though I’m trying.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced these difficulties consistently throughout your childhood and into adulthood?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think it’s been pretty consistent. Like I said, even in elementary school teachers would move my desk or remind me to stay seated because I kept popping up or talking before I was called on. I’d also rush through work even when I understood it. It’s sort of always been there, just looks a little different now.\n\nDoctor: As a child, did you engage in a lot of physical activity, like running around or climbing, more than other kids?\n\nPatient: I guess not so much climbing or anything like that… but I do remember feeling like I had to move a lot. Even if I was sitting there, I’d feel kind of restless inside, like I needed to do something with my hands or switch to something else.\n\nDoctor: Have these difficulties significantly impacted your ability to function or caused substantial distress in your daily life?\n\nPatient: I wouldn’t say it’s… severe, exactly. But yeah, it’s started to affect things. People at work and at home have to follow up with me more than they used to, and that creates some tension. I worry I’m becoming the unreliable one with planning stuff, even though I am trying.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. I might talk more if I’m comfortable or if it’s work-related, but I haven’t really noticed myself dominating conversations.\n\nDoctor: Do you often have trouble finishing tasks or projects you start?\n\nPatient: Sometimes, yeah. Like if my husband asks me to call the insurance company, upload a form, and then email the confirmation… I’ll do the call, maybe the upload, and then the last step just sits there until he reminds me.\n\nDoctor: Do you often have trouble organizing your time or tasks?\n\nPatient: Yeah… somewhat. I have stuff in too many places—Slack, sticky notes, email, my phone. Work tickets, house repair things, budget reminders… it gets scattered, and then I spend more time figuring out what I was supposed to do than just doing it.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel the need to be physically active, like running, climbing, or engaging in other intense physical activities?\n\nPatient: Not like running or climbing, no. It’s more… I can be sitting still, but inside I feel kind of wound up, like I need to move or switch tasks or do something with my hands. Otherwise I get really uncomfortable.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself easily distracted by things around you?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure. I mean… I notice things, but I don’t know if it’s more than most people. It depends on the situation, I guess.\n\nDoctor: Have you always felt this way, even when you were a kid?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I think so. I mean, in elementary school I remember teachers moving my desk sometimes, or telling me to stay seated. I’d pop up a lot, talk before they called on me, or rush through work even when I knew how to do it.\n\nDoctor: Do you often have strong emotional reactions to situations?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… not especially, I guess. I can get irritated sometimes, but I wouldn’t say I’ve really noticed big emotional reactions.\n\nDoctor: Have these difficulties you're describing caused any problems for you at work, at home, or in your relationships with others?\n\nPatient: Yeah… a little. It’s not like everything is falling apart, but at work and at home people have been having to check in with me more. I can tell it’s causing some friction. I worry they’re starting to see me as the unreliable one with planning things, even though I’m trying.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced these difficulties consistently throughout your childhood and into adulthood?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think it’s been pretty consistent. Like I said, even in elementary school teachers would move my desk or remind me to stay seated because I kept popping up or talking before I was called on. I’d also rush through work even when I understood it. It’s sort of always been there, just looks a little different now.\n\nDoctor: As a child, did you engage in a lot of physical activity, like running around or climbing, more than other kids?\n\nPatient: I guess not so much climbing or anything like that… but I do remember feeling like I had to move a lot. Even if I was sitting there, I’d feel kind of restless inside, like I needed to do something with my hands or switch to something else.\n\nDoctor: Have these difficulties significantly impacted your ability to function or caused substantial distress in your daily life?\n\nPatient: I wouldn’t say it’s… severe, exactly. But yeah, it’s started to affect things. People at work and at home have to follow up with me more than they used to, and that creates some tension. I worry I’m becoming the unreliable one with planning stuff, even though I am trying.\n\nDoctor: Have your difficulties caused problems in your relationships with friends or family members?\n\nPatient: A little, yeah. Mostly at home, and sometimes with friends if there’s planning involved. People end up having to remind me or follow up more, and I can tell it gets frustrating. I don’t mean to be unreliable… but I worry that’s how it’s coming across.\n\nDoctor: Have your difficulties significantly affected your relationships with friends or caused you to avoid social situations?\n\nPatient: Not really avoid social situations, no. It’s more that… planning things with friends or at home can get tense because people have to follow up with me, and I feel bad about that. I worry I’m becoming the unreliable one, even when I’m trying.\n\nDoctor: Have your difficulties significantly interfered with your ability to perform your job or maintain relationships?\n\nPatient: Yeah… somewhat. I’m still managing, but it’s creating friction at work and at home. People have to follow up with me more than they used to, especially around planning things, and I worry I’m starting to seem unreliable even though I’m trying.\n\nDoctor: Have your difficulties ever led to the loss of a friendship or a significant strain in a relationship that was important to you?\n\nPatient: I wouldn’t say I’ve lost a friendship because of it. But there’s been strain, yeah… mostly with people close to me. At home, especially, and some planning with friends. They have to follow up more than they used to, and I can tell it wears on them. I worry I’m becoming the unreliable one, even though I’m trying.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. I worry I’m becoming the unreliable one, even when I’m trying.\n\nDoctor: Have your difficulties significantly interfered with your ability to perform your job or maintain relationships?\n\nPatient: Yeah… somewhat. I’m still managing, but it’s creating friction at work and at home.
